Summary Sheet

<!-- b000009 | prose | pages [1] -->
Symphony of Eco-Agriculture:

<!-- b000010 | prose | pages [1] -->
A New Music of Harmonious Coexistence

<!-- b000011 | heading | pages [1] -->
Summary

<!-- b000012 | prose | pages [1] -->
The global expansion of agricultural land has led to problems such as ecosystem imbal- ance, biodiversity loss, and reduced agricultural productivity. To address these challenges, we developed two models: Model I, the Forest-to-Agriculture Transition Ecosystem (FATE)

<!-- b000013 | title | pages [1] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b000014 | prose | pages [1] -->
Model; Model II, the Comprehensive Organic Management and Planning (COMAP) Model.

<!-- b000015 | prose | pages [1] -->
In Model I, the biggest innovation is the combination of the Lotka-Volterra model with a generalized Logistic model, incorporating a spatial diffusion term to describe population migration. We also examined the impacts of herbicides, insecticides, and fertilizers. Based on Assumption 1, we introduced a seasonal variation function to refine the agricultural cycle in the original model. Comparing ecosystem dynamics under different chemical use scenarios (e.g., no insecticides, no fertilizers, or all three chemicals), we found that the absence of in- secticides led to uncontrolled pest populations and ecosystem collapse.

<!-- b000016 | title | pages [1] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b000017 | prose | pages [1] -->
Subsequently, we used Model I to explore the effects of species regression through multi- scenario simulations combined with probability estimations, focusing on producers (shrubs), secondary consumers (larks), and pollinators (bees). Among these, Scenario C (larks and shrubs) performed best, as larks enhanced system stability through pest control, while shrubs supported ecological diversity and stable crop growth.

<!-- b000018 | prose | pages [1] -->
Additionally, we analyzed two human interventions: the herbicide-reduction strategy and a biodiversity assessment considering bats' pollination function. Results showed that reducing herbicide usage improved wheat population growth and ecosystem resilience. Bats signifi- cantly enhanced wheat productivity, achieving a system stability and resilience index of 6.42, compared to 5.01 for frogs.

<!-- b000019 | prose | pages [1] -->
For Model II, our greatest highlight is to solve the multi-objective optimization problem by using the Convex Reformulation Method to trade-off the ecological benefits (biodiversity and system stability) with the economic benefits (agricultural yield and revenue). After prov- ing the model’s concave nature, we solved it using the pairwise transformation and ε-constraint methods. The ecological target value reached 243.58, indicating improvements in biodiversity and stability, while the economic target value was 13,484.36 USD/month, demonstrating ag- ricultural viability. The pest density weighting coefficient (0.45) emphasized its critical role, while the trade-off coefficient (0.57) indicated a bias toward ecological optimization.

<!-- b000020 | prose | pages [1] -->
Finally, sensitivity analyses on Model I revealed that species mortality had a small influ- ence on model stability, while insecticide parameters were particularly sensitive to system performance. Based on these findings, we propose optimizations for farmers

<!-- b000021 | furniture | pages [1] -->


<!-- b000022 | keywords | pages [1] -->
Keywords: FATE Model; COMAP Model; Lotka-Volterra Equation; Eco-Agriculture；

<!-- b000023 | prose | pages [1] -->

1 Introduction

<!-- b000034 | heading | pages [3] -->
1.1 Background

<!-- b000035 | furniture | pages [3] -->


<!-- b000036 | furniture | pages [3] -->


<!-- b000037 | furniture | pages [3] -->


<!-- b000038 | furniture | pages [3] -->


<!-- b000039 | furniture | pages [3] -->


<!-- b000040 | furniture | pages [3] -->
Page 3 of 25

<!-- b000041 | prose | pages [3] -->
The growing global demand for agriculture has driven the large-scale conversion of for- ests into agricultural land and the transformation of complex ecosystems into monoculture farming landscapes. This shift has resulted in challenges such as soil degradation and pest out- breaks, compelling farmers to rely heavily on chemicals (e.g., insecticides and herbicides) to maintain crop yields. However, prolonged chemical use disrupts ecological balance, diminishes native species diversity, and undermines agroecosystem sustainability [1]. To address these challenges, the introduction of beneficial insects (e.g., bats and bees) has been shown to restore ecological balance, improve crop productivity, and enhance soil health [2]. Balancing agricul-

<!-- b000042 | prose | pages [3] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b000043 | prose | pages [3] -->
tural development with ecological conservation remains a critical global concern. Accordingly,

<!-- b000044 | heading | pages [3] -->
we utilize ecosystem dynamic modeling and decision-making simulations (Figure 1) to pro-

<!-- b000045 | prose | pages [3] -->
vide scientific insights for policy formulation and promote the sustainable development of eco- friendly agriculture.

<!-- b000046 | prose | pages [3] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b000047 | furniture | pages [3] -->


<!-- b000048 | caption | pages [3] -->
Figure 1: Conversion of Forest Ecology to Agroecology

<!-- b000049 | heading | pages [3] -->
1.2 Restatement of the Problem

<!-- b000050 | prose | pages [3] -->
Given the above context, we aim to analyze ecosystem dynamics and their interactions with anthropogenic decisions following the conversion of forests into agricultural land by con- structing a mathematical model. Our study focuses on the following aspects:

<!-- b000051 | heading | pages [3] -->
 Natural Process Analysis: Develop an ecosystem dynamic model to capture ecological

<!-- b000052 | prose | pages [3] -->
changes after forest conversion into agricultural land. This includes establishing the food web structure, evaluating the impacts of agricultural cycles and chemical use on plant and consumer populations, and assessing the role of species reintroduction in eco- logical restoration within marginal habitats (Tasks I & II).  Anthropogenic Decision-Making Assessment: Investigate the effects of various agri-

<!-- b000053 | prose | pages [3] -->
cultural management decisions on ecosystems, including the role of chemical reduction and the introduction of key species (e.g., bats and bees) in pest control and system sta- bility. Additionally, simulate the combined impacts of green agricultural practices on ecosystem services, crop productivity, and economic benefits (Tasks III & IV).

<!-- b000054 | heading | pages [3] -->
 Policy Recommendation Development: Based on the modeling results, propose eco-

<!-- b000055 | prose | pages [3] -->
friendly agricultural strategies that balance economic viability and ecological sustaina-

bility. Furthermore, explore the potential of policy incentives to promote green agricul- tural practices (Task V).

<!-- b000072 | heading | pages [4] -->
1.3 Literature Review

<!-- b000073 | prose | pages [4] -->
Forest ecosystems feature complex food chains and high species diversity, with soil health relying on natural cycles [3]. Interactions among plants, herbivores, carnivores, and decompos- ers are vital for maintaining balance. In contrast, agroecosystems, dominated by monocrops with low biodiversity, are further disrupted by fertilizers and insecticides [4].

<!-- b000074 | prose | pages [4] -->
Agricultural transformation is influenced by natural evolution and human activities. Herb- icides and insecticides affect crop health and pest dynamics, while species like bats support recovery through pest control and pollination. Similarly, bird reintroduction aids in restoring ecological balance [5]. Organic agriculture provides a sustainable alternative by reducing chemical inputs and enhancing soil health and diversity, though short-term challenges in yield and cost remain.

<!-- b000075 | prose | pages [4] -->
Common ecological and food web models include the Logistic model, Lotka-Volterra model, and food web model, with their respective strengths and limitations shown in Figure 2.

<!-- b000076 | furniture | pages [4] -->


<!-- b000077 | caption | pages [4] -->
Figure 2: Literature Review Chart

<!-- b000080 | heading | pages [4] -->
2 Assumptions and Justifications

<!-- b000081 | prose | pages [4] -->
In order to simplify the problem and improve the model's tractability and computational efficiency, we make the following assumptions:

<!-- b000082 | heading | pages [4] -->
Assumption 1: Crops are predominantly seasonal, and the agricultural cycle aligns with

<!-- b000083 | prose | pages [4] -->
seasonal variations.

<!-- b000084 | heading | pages [4] -->
Justification: This assumption is based on the growth characteristics of seasonal crops,

<!-- b000085 | prose | pages [4] -->
where major agricultural activities in agroecosystems (e.g., sowing, growing, fertilizing, har- vesting) are driven by seasonal climatic conditions. By aligning the agricultural cycle with seasonal variations, the model is simplified, allowing the use of a uniform periodic function to describe both agricultural cycles and seasonal changes, thereby enhancing the rationality of dynamic modeling.

<!-- b000086 | heading | pages [4] -->
Assumption 2: Herbicides are primarily used during the early stages of agroecosystems

<!-- b000087 | prose | pages [4] -->
to control weed expansion.

<!-- b000088 | furniture | pages [4] -->


<!-- b000089 | prose | pages [4] -->
关注数学模型

<!-- b000090 | furniture | pages [4] -->

<!-- b000091 | furniture | pages [4] -->

Page 5 of 25

<!-- b000107 | prose | pages [5] -->
Justification: During the transition from forests to agroecosystems, legacy weeds quickly adapt and compete for farmland resources. To ensure optimal crop growth and reduce compe- tition from weeds, farmers commonly use herbicides to manage agricultural systems during their establishment phase. This reflects a widely observed pattern in agricultural practices.

<!-- b000108 | prose | pages [5] -->
Assumption 3: The actual study area has a spherical quadrilateral distribution but is sim- plified to a planar rectangular projection in the model. This simplification does not significantly affect the accuracy of the model results.

<!-- b000109 | prose | pages [5] -->
Justification: Given the relatively small size of the study area, the impact of latitude and longitude changes on spherical distances can be precisely calculated using the Haversine for- mula. Simplifying the sphere to a planar rectangular projection reduces model complexity while maintaining sufficient geographic accuracy. This ensures the scientific validity of model analyses and results.

<!-- b000110 | prose | pages [5] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b000111 | prose | pages [5] -->
Assumption 4: It is assumed that all data collected are reliable. Justification: This assumption ensures the accuracy of the data input to the model and avoids analytical bias due to data errors, thus allowing the study to focus more on the model mechanism itself.

<!-- b000112 | prose | pages [5] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

web by integrating the Lotka-Volterra and generalized Logistic models to describe energy

flows among producers, consumers, and decomposers. Agricultural cycles, seasonal variations, and environmental variables were then incorporated to quantify, from a spatiotemporal per- spective, the combined effects of agricultural activities and chemical use on population dynam-

<!-- b000822 | prose | pages [7] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b000823 | prose | pages [7] -->
ics and ecosystem stability.

<!-- b000824 | heading | pages [7] -->
4.2 Multi-Trophic Food Web

<!-- b000825 | prose | pages [7] -->
The multi-trophic food web we constructed includes primary producers, primary con- sumers, secondary consumers, tertiary consumers, and decomposers, while incorporating the dynamic effects of herbicides, insecticides, and fertilizers. Primary producers form the foundation, supplying energy that flows upward through trophic levels, while decomposers recycle nutrients by breaking down organic matter. Predation and energy transfer govern the interactions between these levels, maintaining ecological balance. Figure 5 provides a sche- matic representation of these relationships.

<!-- b000826 | prose | pages [7] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b000827 | furniture | pages [7] -->


<!-- b000828 | caption | pages [7] -->
Figure 5: Map of Food Web Relationships

<!-- b000838 | prose | pages [7] -->
( 2 )

<!-- b000839 | prose | pages [7] -->
diffusion coefficient reflecting the rate of population diffusion in space, and ∇2𝑃𝑃(𝑥𝑥, 𝑡𝑡) is the

<!-- b000840 | prose | pages [7] -->
∇2𝑃𝑃= ∂2𝑃𝑃

<!-- b000841 | prose | pages [7] -->
∂𝑥𝑥2 + ∂2𝑃𝑃

<!-- b000842 | prose | pages [7] -->
Laplace operator, defined as:

<!-- b000843 | prose | pages [7] -->
∂𝑦𝑦2  (in two dimensions) , ( 3 )

<!-- b000844 | prose | pages [7] -->
which represents the spatial gradient of changes in population density. The physical interpreta- tion of this diffusion term is that the temporal change in population density is determined by the spatial non-uniformity of its distribution. The full derivation of this formula is provided in Appendix Proof 1.

<!-- b000845 | heading | pages [7] -->

<!-- b001576 | prose | pages [8] -->
such as ladybug beetles, indirectly weakening pest control. Additionally, insecticide res- idues can harm decomposers, lowering decomposition efficiency and compromising soil stability. Ecological Impacts of Fertilizers: Fertilizers directly enhance crop growth and competi-

<!-- b001577 | prose | pages [8] -->
tiveness by enriching soil nutrients. However, overuse can disrupt the chemical balance of the soil, inhibit decomposer activity, reduce microbial diversity, and ultimately affect overall ecosystem health. Based on the multi-trophic structure of the ecosystem and the multifaceted impacts of chemical dynamics, we formulated population dynamics equations for each trophic level. The detailed equations are as follows.

<!-- b001578 | prose | pages [8] -->

<!-- b001642 | prose | pages [9] -->

decline caused by the non-target effects of insecticides. The governing equation is as follows:

<!-- b003096 | prose | pages [12] -->
grows significantly while weeds decline. Fertilizers enhance wheat competitiveness, and herbicides effectively control weed spread. Moderate Use Stage (t=60): With sustained moderate chemical use, the wheat population

<!-- b003097 | prose | pages [12] -->
stabilizes at a high level, and weeds remain suppressed. The ecosystem achieves a dynamic balance between decomposers and soil nutrients, supporting long-term development. Overuse Stage (t=96): Excessive chemical application disrupts ecosystem balance, reducing

<!-- b003098 | prose | pages [12] -->
decomposer activity and negatively affecting soil nutrient cycling. While wheat populations remain high, ecosystem stability is threatened, and weed populations are nearly eradicated.

<!-- b003101 | heading | pages [12] -->
4.3 Results of Model I

<!-- b003102 | prose | pages [12] -->
To further investigate the impact of chemical agents on the population dynamics of various species in agroecosystems, we simulated three scenarios: no insecticide use, no fertilizer use, and the combined use of all three chemicals. (Based on Assumption 2, herbicides are required to control weed expansion caused by forest-to-farmland conversion to ensure a suitable growth environment for crops.) The effects of these scenarios on the temporal dynamics of all species in the food web were analyzed (Figures 9–11).

<!-- b003103 | furniture | pages [12] -->


<!-- b003104 | furniture | pages [12] -->


<!-- b003105 | caption | pages [12] -->
Figure 9: Full chemical usage. Changes in consumer populations (left); changes in crop

<!-- b003106 | prose | pages [12] -->
populations (right).

<!-- b003126 | prose | pages [14] -->
we further conducted multi-scenario simulations to dynamically analyze the ecological ef- fects of pairwise species reintroduction combinations, quantifying their combined impacts on ecosystem stability, species diversity, and crop yield.

<!-- b003127 | heading | pages [14] -->
5.1 Species Distribution Model

<!-- b003128 | prose | pages [14] -->
The species distribution model is a tool used to predict species reintroduction probabilities based on environmental variables. It quantifies the influence of environmental factors on spe- cies distribution and provides dynamic predictions.

<!-- b003129 | heading | pages [14] -->
5.1.1 Probability Calculation Method

<!-- b003130 | prose | pages [14] -->
Generalized Linear Models (GLM) or Generalized Additive Models (GAM) are com- monly used to describe the relationship between reintroduction probability and environmental variables. The model is expressed as follows:

<!-- b003131 | prose | pages [14] -->
𝑛𝑛

<!-- b003132 | prose | pages [14] -->
𝑃𝑃( return ) = 𝑙𝑙𝑙𝑙𝑙𝑙𝑙𝑙𝑙𝑙−1 ൭𝛽𝛽0 + ෍

<!-- b003133 | prose | pages [14] -->
𝛽𝛽𝑖𝑖𝑋𝑋𝑖𝑖൱

<!-- b003134 | prose | pages [14] -->
𝑖𝑖=1

<!-- b003135 | prose | pages [14] -->
Where 𝑃𝑃 (return) represents the probability of a species reintroducing in a given year. logit−1 is the inverse function of the logistic regression. 𝛽𝛽0 is the intercept term. 𝛽𝛽𝑖𝑖 denotes the re- gression coefficient for the environmental variable 𝑋𝑋𝑖𝑖. 𝑋𝑋𝑖𝑖 represents environmental variables, which include: 𝑋𝑋1 : Habitat maturity. 𝑋𝑋2 : Climate conditions. 𝑋𝑋3 : Intensity of chemical usage. 𝑋𝑋4 : Intensity of agricultural activities. 𝑋𝑋5 : Time.

<!-- b003136 | heading | pages [14] -->
5.1.2 Calculation Results and Evaluation

<!-- b003137 | prose | pages [14] -->
Using the Species Distribution Model (SDM), we simulated and calculated the annual reintroduction probabilities of bees, larks, and shrubs over the next 10 years, along with an analysis of the model's performance metrics, as illustrated in Figure 12.

<!-- b003138 | furniture | pages [14] -->


<!-- b003139 | caption | pages [14] -->
Figure 12: Predicted Results and Model Evaluations

<!-- b003140 | prose | pages [14] -->
The figure illustrates the reintroduction probabilities of three species (Shrub, Lark, Bee) over 10 years, along with the model's comprehensive evaluation. The line chart shows dynamic changes in reintroduction probabilities, with bees maintaining relatively stable and high prob- abilities, while shrubs and larks exhibit greater fluctuations, reflecting their higher sensitivity to environmental factors and spatiotemporal conditions.

<!-- b003141 | prose | pages [14] -->
The model evaluation results indicate high predictive accuracy for all three species (as- sessment scores above 0.78), with bees performing slightly better than shrubs and larks. This

TEAM # 2508861

<!-- b003887 | heading | pages [15] -->
5.2 Multi-Scenario Simulations

<!-- b003888 | furniture | pages [15] -->


<!-- b003889 | furniture | pages [15] -->


<!-- b003890 | furniture | pages [15] -->


<!-- b003891 | furniture | pages [15] -->


<!-- b003892 | furniture | pages [15] -->


<!-- b003893 | furniture | pages [15] -->
Page 15 of 25

<!-- b003894 | prose | pages [15] -->
To analyze the impact of species reintroduction on the current ecosystem, we conducted pairwise scenario analyses involving bees, larks, and shrubs. The focus was on exploring inter- actions between species and their effects on ecosystem dynamics.

<!-- b003895 | prose | pages [15] -->
Scenario A: Bees (Pollinators) and Shrubs (Producers)

<!-- b003896 | prose | pages [15] -->
Scenario A: Bees and Shrubs. The dynamics of shrubs and bees are modeled by incorpo-

<!-- b003897 | prose | pages [15] -->

<!-- b003898 | prose | pages [15] -->
𝜕𝜕𝑃𝑃shrub

<!-- b003899 | prose | pages [15] -->
rating the positive feedback of pollination and resource availability:

<!-- b003935 | heading | pages [15] -->
5.3 Scenario Simulation Results

<!-- b003936 | prose | pages [15] -->
( 17 )

<!-- b003937 | furniture | pages [15] -->


<!-- b003938 | prose | pages [15] -->
Modeling and analysis of species reintroduction under different scenarios revealed that after introducing new species at t=24, ecological dynamics gradually stabilized (Figure 13), highlighting the regulatory role of reintroduced species. At t=84, crop populations exhibited exponential growth, demonstrating the long-term positive effects of species reintroduction on system stability and productivity.

<!-- b003939 | prose | pages [15] -->
Scenario A (Bees and Shrubs): Bee pollination had minimal impact on crop growth, as

<!-- b003940 | prose | pages [15] -->
competition between shrubs and crops for resources limited population growth, making this the weakest scenario. Scenario B (Larks and Bees): The synergy of larks and bees boosted crop growth during

<!-- b003941 | prose | pages [15] -->
exponential phases, but initial system instability and fluctuations were observed. Scenario C (Larks and Shrubs): Larks controlled pests, and shrubs supported ecological

<!-- b003942 | prose | pages [15] -->
diversity, enabling steady crop growth and making this the most optimal scenario for long-

<!-- b004647 | caption | pages [16] -->
Figure 13: Crop Dynamics Under Different Scenarios

<!-- b004648 | prose | pages [16] -->
Under different scenarios, the dynamic effects of species reintroduction on native species are shown in Figures 14–16. The model results indicate that the introduction of reintroduced species significantly disrupted and regulated the population dynamics of native consumers.

<!-- b004649 | furniture | pages [16] -->


<!-- b004650 | furniture | pages [16] -->


<!-- b004651 | furniture | pages [16] -->


<!-- b004652 | caption | pages [16] -->
Figure 15: Scenario B

<!-- b004653 | caption | pages [16] -->
Figure 14: Scenario A

<!-- b004654 | caption | pages [16] -->
Figure 16: Scenario C

<!-- b004655 | prose | pages [16] -->
Scenario A highlights the competitive effects among secondary consumers. The reintro-

<!-- b004656 | prose | pages [16] -->
duction of secondary consumers reduced predation pressure on primary consumers but also led to inhibitory interactions between populations. Scenario B reflects the complex impact of diversified reintroduction of secondary con-

<!-- b004657 | prose | pages [16] -->
sumers on energy flow within the system, resulting in significant fluctuations in consumer populations. Scenario C demonstrates the significant regulatory effect of reintroduced species on pri-

<!-- b004658 | prose | pages [16] -->
mary consumer populations, particularly in suppressing pest population fluctuations, which indirectly enhanced the competitive advantage of crops.

<!-- b004659 | heading | pages [16] -->
6 Analysis of Chemical Dependency and Biodiversity Synergy

<!-- b004660 | prose | pages [16] -->
To investigate the impact of human decisions on agroecosystems, we analyzed two aspects: reducing chemical dependency and enhancing biodiversity synergy. Regarding chemical de- pendency, based on Assumption 2, herbicides are deemed necessary during the early stages of forest-to-farmland conversion to control weeds. However, as the ecosystem matures, herb- icide use should be gradually reduced and eventually discontinued. For biodiversity syn- ergy, we extended bats' ecological role to include pollination and introduced frogs as an alter- native species. A quantitative analysis of the stability and resilience of both species was con- ducted to assess their respective contributions.

<!-- b004661 | heading | pages [16] -->
6.1 Herbicide Dependency Reduction

<!-- b004662 | prose | pages [16] -->
For modeling the reduction of herbicide dependency, we based our approach on Assump- tion 2, which states that in the first year of the agroecosystem, herbicides are fully relied upon

<!-- b004663 | prose | pages [16] -->
关注数学模型

<!-- b004664 | prose | pages [16] -->
to control weed expansion. It is assumed that the herbicide usage remains constant in the second

<!-- b004698 | heading | pages [17] -->
6.2 Biodiversity Synergy

<!-- b004699 | prose | pages [17] -->
For modeling biodiversity synergy, we introduced bats as pollinators to enhance crop

<!-- b004700 | prose | pages [17] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b004701 | prose | pages [17] -->
productivity. To achieve this, we modified the crop productivity function in the producer model to incorporate the positive contribution of pollination efficiency. Additionally, to analyze the role of alternative species, we selected frogs as a substitute to study their performance in pest control and ecological balance restoration. Finally, to comprehensively quantify the impact of bats and frogs on agroecosystems, we used a system resilience index to analyze the recovery time and dynamic characteristics of the system after disturbances.

<!-- b005449 | heading | pages [18] -->
6.3 Calculated Results

<!-- b005450 | prose | pages [18] -->
We evaluated the dynamic effects of the herbicide dependency reduction strategy and bi- odiversity synergy on crop and consumer populations, as shown in Figures 17 and 18.

<!-- b005451 | furniture | pages [18] -->


<!-- b005452 | caption | pages [18] -->
Figure 17: Herbicide attenuation results. In this figure, A represents changes in crop popu-

<!-- b005453 | prose | pages [18] -->
lation dynamics under different herbicide use strategies; B and C represent changes in con- sumer population dynamics using the herbicide attenuation strategy and the original strategy, respectively.

<!-- b005454 | furniture | pages [18] -->


<!-- b005455 | caption | pages [18] -->
Figure 18: Biodiversity Synergy Results

<!-- b005456 | prose | pages [18] -->
Figure 17(A) shows the impact of herbicide usage on crop population dynamics. During

<!-- b005457 | prose | pages [18] -->
关注数学模型

<!-- b005458 | prose | pages [18] -->
the full dependency phase (0–24 months), crop growth was significantly suppressed. In the

<!-- b005459 | furniture | pages [18] -->

<!-- b005477 | prose | pages [19] -->
gradual reduction phase (25–60 months), crops steadily recovered and exhibited exponential growth after 70 months. This demonstrates that reducing herbicide usage moderately can en- hance ecosystem support capacity and mitigate environmental impacts. Figures 17(B) and

<!-- b005478 | prose | pages [19] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b005479 | prose | pages [19] -->
17(C) compare consumer populations under different strategies. The reduction strategy (Fig- ure 17(B)) reduced population fluctuations and improved long-term stability, while the original

<!-- b005480 | heading | pages [19] -->
strategy (Figure 17(C)) caused greater fluctuations and lower stability. These results highlight

<!-- b005481 | prose | pages [19] -->
the importance of herbicide reduction in maintaining ecological balance and resilience.

<!-- b005482 | caption | pages [19] -->
Figure 18 compares the effects of bats and frogs on system stability. Bats significantly

<!-- b005483 | prose | pages [19] -->
improved crop productivity through pollination, with minimal fluctuations and a resilience in- dex of 6.42, indicating strong recovery capability. Frogs contributed primarily to pest control but caused greater fluctuations due to their impact on soil and decomposers, with a resilience index of 5.01, reflecting lower stability. Overall, bats demonstrated superior ecosystem stability

<!-- b005484 | prose | pages [19] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b005485 | prose | pages [19] -->
and sustainability, offering valuable insights for species introduction strategies.

<!-- b005486 | heading | pages [19] -->
7 Model II: Comprehensive Organic Management and Planning

<!-- b005487 | prose | pages [19] -->
In green agricultural practices, adopting organic farming methods is essential for under- standing their comprehensive impacts on ecosystems and individual components, including pest control, crop health, plant reproduction, biodiversity, sustainability, and economic benefits. To address these, we developed the Comprehensive Organic Management and

<!-- b005488 | heading | pages [19] -->
Planning (COMAP) model, employing multi-objective nonlinear programming to analyze the

<!-- b005489 | prose | pages [19] -->
ecological and economic effects of different organic farming scenarios.

<!-- b005490 | heading | pages [19] -->
7.1 Model Preparation

<!-- b005491 | prose | pages [19] -->
Before constructing the model, we systematically analyzed various organic farming sce- narios to assess their potential impacts on ecosystems and agricultural production. These sce- narios were based on six key dimensions: pest control, crop health, plant reproduction, biodi- versity, long-term sustainability, and economic benefits.

<!-- b005492 | prose | pages [19] -->
Scenario 1: A system without chemical inputs, relying solely on natural ecological bal- ance and biodiversity for pest control and crop reproduction. While this may cause short-term pest population fluctuations, it enhances biodiversity and soil health over the long term.

<!-- b005493 | prose | pages [19] -->
Scenario 2: Combines organic fertilizers and biological control measures, using lim- ited external inputs to improve crop yields while reducing dependence on natural ecosystems.

<!-- b005494 | prose | pages [19] -->
Scenario 3: Explores the synergy between diversified cropping systems and ecologi- cal conservation, implementing practices such as crop rotation, intercropping, and edge habitat construction to optimize ecological and economic outcomes.

<!-- b005495 | prose | pages [19] -->
These analyses provided essential parameter support for constructing the COMAP model, forming the theoretical basis for designing objective functions and defining constraints.

<!-- b005496 | heading | pages [19] -->
7.2 Model Construction

<!-- b005497 | prose | pages [19] -->
(1) Decision Variables To optimize the ecological and economic benefits under organic farming scenarios, we define the following decision variables to quantify key strategies and resource allocations in

<!-- b005498 | prose | pages [19] -->
𝑥𝑥organic (𝑡𝑡)  : The amount of organic fertilizer input chosen by farmers at time 𝑡𝑡 .

<!-- b005499 | prose | pages [19] -->
agricultural practices:

<!-- b005500 | prose | pages [19] -->

<!-- b006912 | prose | pages [20] -->
(3) Constraints

<!-- b006913 | prose | pages [20] -->
( 24 )

<!-- b006914 | prose | pages [20] -->
To optimize the sustainability of agroecosystems, we propose four constraints encompass- ing chemical usage, resource allocation, biodiversity, and soil nutrient balance.

<!-- b006915 | prose | pages [20] -->
 Constraint 1: Biodiversity Protection. This constraint ensures that the total popu-

<!-- b006916 | prose | pages [20] -->
lation of species within the ecosystem remains above the minimum biodiversity threshold to maintain ecological health:

<!-- b006917 | prose | pages [20] -->
𝑛𝑛

<!-- b006918 | prose | pages [20] -->
𝑁𝑁𝑖𝑖(𝑡𝑡) ≥𝑛𝑛min,

<!-- b006919 | prose | pages [20] -->
෍

<!-- b006920 | prose | pages [20] -->
𝑖𝑖=1

<!-- b006921 | prose | pages [20] -->
( 25 )

<!-- b006922 | prose | pages [20] -->
where 𝑁𝑁𝑖𝑖(𝑡𝑡) : Population size of species 𝑖𝑖 at time 𝑡𝑡. 𝑛𝑛min: Minimum biodiversity threshold.

<!-- b006923 | prose | pages [20] -->
 Constraint 2: Soil Nutrient Balance. The deviation between actual and optimal soil

<!-- b006924 | prose | pages [20] -->
nutrient levels must remain within an allowable range to ensure that soil functions

<!-- b006925 | prose | pages [20] -->

<!-- b007044 | heading | pages [21] -->
8 Sensitivity Analysis

<!-- b007045 | prose | pages [21] -->
The parameter perturbation method is a commonly used approach to evaluate the sensi- tivity of model outputs to changes in input parameters. For our FATE model, we applied this method to conduct sensitivity analysis. Specifically, we selected fertilizers, insecticides, and species mortality rates as perturbation parameters. Sensitivity was assessed using two ap- proaches: single-parameter perturbation and multi-parameter mixed perturbation. The model responses under different scenarios were simulated, and the sensitivity was quantified using the Standardized Sensitivity Coefficient (SSC), defined as:

<!-- b007061 | heading | pages [23] -->
10 Conclusion

<!-- b007062 | prose | pages [23] -->
Using the FATE and COMAP models, we analyzed the impacts of chemical use, species regression, biodiversity synergy, and organic agriculture optimization on agroecosystems. Op- timized chemical strategies were critical for balancing ecological stability and crop productiv- ity, while species reintroduction required pest management to enhance long-term stability. Gradual herbicide reduction promoted crop growth and stabilized consumer populations, with bats showing higher resilience through pollination compared to frogs' pest control. The CO- MAP model effectively balanced ecological and economic goals, highlighting the importance

<!-- b007063 | prose | pages [23] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b007064 | prose | pages [23] -->
of biodiversity and pest density in system performance. These findings provide valuable in- sights for sustainable agricultural management.

System Stability 𝐷𝐷(𝑡𝑡) = −ln (𝜎𝜎2(𝑡𝑡) + 1) : Variance 𝜎𝜎2(𝑡𝑡) is a convex function, and −ln (𝑥𝑥) is concave. Therefore, 𝐷𝐷(𝑡𝑡) is concave. (2) Concavity of 𝐹𝐹2 𝐹𝐹2 is defined as the difference between crop yield, market revenue, and cost: Crop Yield 𝑃𝑃wheats (𝑡𝑡) = ln (1 + 𝑁𝑁wheats (𝑡𝑡)) : Since ln (𝑥𝑥) is concave, 𝑃𝑃wheats (𝑡𝑡) is concave. Market Revenue 𝑅𝑅market (𝑡𝑡) : Given that crop market prices are relatively stable, 𝑅𝑅market (𝑡𝑡) can be approximated as a linear function and is convex. Cost 𝐶𝐶(𝑡𝑡) = 𝛽𝛽𝑡𝑡2 : 𝐶𝐶(𝑡𝑡) is convex, but its negative −𝐶𝐶(𝑡𝑡) is concave. By combining these terms, 𝑭𝑭𝟐𝟐 is concave.

Topic: Recommendations for Organic Farming Practices Form：Team # 2508861 of 2025 ICM Date：January 28, 2025 To our farmer friends Dear Farmers,

<!-- b007151 | prose | pages [25] -->
Sustainable organic agriculture can not only effectively control pests, improve soil health, and enhance biodiversity, but also create economic benefits in the ever-expanding organic market. Therefore, we have specially written this letter to provide you with recommendations for exploring organic farming practices.

<!-- b007152 | prose | pages [25] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b007153 | prose | pages [25] -->
The transition to organic farming requires initial investments, such as organic fertilizers and biological

<!-- b007154 | prose | pages [25] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b007155 | prose | pages [25] -->
pest control. These measures effectively control pests, reduce the use of chemicals, and enhance soil fertility, leading to long-term returns. Its sustainability is reflected in improved ecological resilience, enhanced soil productivity, and reduced environmental impact. Therefore, a positive cycle between economics and sustainability requires our collective effort.

<!-- b007156 | prose | pages [25] -->
We sincerely hope that these suggestions will help you find the best balance between economics and ecology in practice, and together, we can work towards a healthier agricultural ecosystem.

<!-- b007157 | prose | pages [25] -->
Yours sincerely,

Query1: What is the difference between forest ecosystems and agro-ecosystems?

<!-- b007178 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
Output:

<!-- b007179 | furniture | pages [26] -->


<!-- b007180 | furniture | pages [26] -->


<!-- b007181 | furniture | pages [26] -->


<!-- b007182 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
There are many significant differences between forest ecosystems and agro-ecosystems

<!-- b007183 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
in terms of structure, function, species diversity and ecological processes. The following are

<!-- b007184 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
their major differences:

<!-- b007185 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
1. Species Diversity

<!-- b007186 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
Forest ecosystems: Forest ecosystems usually have a high species diversity. This in-

<!-- b007187 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
cludes different types of plants, animals, microorganisms, and complex interactions between

<!-- b007188 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
them. Species in forests have strong mutually beneficial symbiotic, competitive and preda-

<!-- b007189 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
tory relationships that form highly complex food chains.

<!-- b007190 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
Agroecosystems: Agroecosystems are more homogenous in species, consisting mainly

<!-- b007191 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
of crops, weeds, pests, and other plants grown or managed by farmers. Species diversity is

<!-- b007192 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
usually low due to high human intervention, and many ecological processes (e.g., pollina-

<!-- b007193 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
tion, soil fertility maintenance, etc.) often depend on external inputs such as fertilizers and

<!-- b007194 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
pesticides.

<!-- b007195 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
2. Ecological Processes

<!-- b007196 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
Forest ecosystems: Forests maintain ecological balance through the natural carbon, ni-

<!-- b007197 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
trogen and water cycles. Forest soils are rich in organic matter, maintain high soil fertility,

<!-- b007198 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
and have a strong ability to regulate water sources. Forests also absorb large amounts of

<!-- b007199 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
carbon dioxide through photosynthesis by trees and plants.

<!-- b007200 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
Agroecosystems: Ecological processes in agroecosystems are usually highly influenced

<!-- b007201 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
by human intervention. Crops have short growth cycles and soil management (e.g., fertili-

<!-- b007202 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
zation, irrigation) has a greater impact on the soil, which can easily lead to soil degradation,

<!-- b007203 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
infertility, or salinization. Agricultural activities usually result in rapid depletion of soil wa-

<!-- b007204 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
ter, nutrients and organic matter.

<!-- b007205 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
3. Productivity and biomass

<!-- b007206 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
Forest ecosystems: Forests typically have high annual productivity and a long-term ca-

<!-- b007207 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
pacity to store carbon. Forests provide abundant biomass, including trees, shrubs, and herbs,

<!-- b007208 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
and material cycling is more stable in forest ecosystems.

<!-- b007209 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
Agricultural ecosystems: The productivity of agricultural land is usually more concen-

<!-- b007210 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
trated and cyclical, and is largely dependent on human management and technology (e.g.,

<!-- b007211 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
irrigation, fertilization, etc.). In contrast to forests, the biomass of farmland is concentrated

<!-- b007212 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
in the crops themselves, and the long-term productivity of the soil often depends on external

<!-- b007213 | ai_disclosure | pages [26] -->
replenishment.

<!-- b007239 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
to ensure the long-term environmental, social and economic sustainability of agricultural

<!-- b007240 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
production. The following are some of the key considerations:

<!-- b007241 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
1. Soil Health and Management

<!-- b007242 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
Organic Fertilizers and Compost: Organic agriculture emphasizes the use of natural

<!-- b007243 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
fertilizers (e.g. compost, green manure, animal manure, etc.) rather than chemical fertilizers

<!-- b007244 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
to increase the organic matter content of the soil and to enhance soil fertility and structure.

<!-- b007245 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
Crop Rotation and Intercropping: Crop rotation (rotation of different crops) and inter-

<!-- b007246 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
cropping (mixing of different crops) help to break the cycle of pests and diseases while

<!-- b007247 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
maintaining a balance of soil nutrients and avoiding over-dependence on a single crop.

<!-- b007248 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
Reducing Soil Erosion: Reducing soil erosion through the use of cover crops, conser-

<!-- b007249 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
vation tillage, etc. improves the water retention capacity and biological activity of the soil.

<!-- b007250 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b007251 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
2. Biodiversity Conservation

<!-- b007252 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
Ecosystem Services: Conservation and enhancement of biodiversity is essential in or-

<!-- b007253 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
ganic agriculture. This includes maintaining and restoring wildlife habitats, promoting spe-

<!-- b007254 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
cies diversity (e.g., beneficial insects, pollinators, etc.), and providing ecological buffer

<!-- b007255 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
strips by planting a diversity of plants along farm boundaries.

<!-- b007256 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
Reducing the Impact of Pests and Diseases: Reduce pest and disease damage without

<!-- b007257 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
relying on chemical pesticides by introducing beneficial insects (e.g., bees, bats, etc.), culti-

<!-- b007258 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
vating pest-resistant crop varieties, and using biopesticides or physical means of control.

<!-- b007259 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
3.Reducing Chemical Dependency

<!-- b007260 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b007261 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
Control of Fertilizer and Pesticide Use: Sustainable organic agriculture requires avoid-

<!-- b007262 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
ing the use of chemical fertilizers and pesticides altogether, and instead managing crop

<!-- b007263 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
growth and controlling pests and diseases through natural methods (e.g., compost, plant-

<!-- b007264 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
based fertilizers, natural pesticides, etc.).

<!-- b007265 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
Natural Enemy Utilization: Control of pest populations with the help of natural preda-

<!-- b007266 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
tors, parasites, or microorganisms, thus reducing reliance on chemicals.

<!-- b007267 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
4.Water Resources Management

<!-- b007268 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
Water Saving Irrigation Technology: Adoption of drip irrigation, sprinkler irrigation,

<!-- b007269 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
and other efficient water resource utilization technologies to reduce water evaporation and

<!-- b007270 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
wastage, and to ensure sustainable use of water for agriculture.

<!-- b007271 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
Rainwater Harvesting and Management: Ensure water supply during the dry season by

<!-- b007272 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
constructing rainwater harvesting systems, cisterns, and other measures.

<!-- b007273 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
Soil water retention: Improve the water retention capacity of the soil and reduce water

<!-- b007274 | ai_disclosure | pages [27] -->
loss by means of cover crops and deep plowing.
